@@128005553 <p> China now accounts for two thirds of all renewable power generation and as the cost of renewable generating capacity tumbles , 87% of all capital expenditure on renewables in 2024 was made in the Global South . / bne IntelliNews <p> By Arnaud Bertrand in SwitzerlandApril 25 , 2025 <p> According to the latest report by the International Renewable Energy Agency ( IRENA ) , an extraordinary 64% -- nearly two-thirds -- of all renewable energy capacity added globally in 2024 was installed in China . <p> At this point , China is almost single-handedly driving the global energy transition . <p> For comparison , the United States -- still the world 's second-largest emitter of CO2 -- was responsible for just 7% of new renewable capacity in 2024 , and now accounts for only 9% of total global renewable energy , against China 's 41% . This gap is likely to widen further under a potential second Trump administration , given his continued dismissal of climate change as a " hoax . " <p> Even if one shares such scepticism , these numbers should provoke serious reflection . @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ ? It 's not out of ideology or " wokeness . " There are several strategic reasons behind this effort . <p> First , China 's leadership recognises the global perception advantage . Even if some in the West question climate change , 86% of the world 's population believes it is a real and urgent issue , according to Our World in Data ( OWID ) . From their perspective , China is positioning itself as a responsible global power -- particularly in contrast to the US . <p> Secondly , economics play a major role . Solar power is now significantly cheaper than electricity generated from fossil fuels . According to IRENA , global solar PV costs in 2023 were 56% lower than fossil fuel and nuclear options . Onshore wind is even more competitive , with costs now 67% lower than fossil-fuel-based alternatives . <p> Most importantly , there 's a long-term strategic calculus at play . Imagine a future where countries like the US persist with fossil-fuel-based economies while China dominates clean energy production . Those holding on to legacy systems will be at a disadvantage @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ China benefits from near-zero marginal energy costs . <p> Beyond price , China also secures energy independence and positions itself for technological and industrial leadership in the dominant energy systems of the future . <p> This places China in a dominant position as a supplier of energy infrastructure to the region that will drive 85% of global electricity demand growth over the coming years , according to the International Energy Agency ( IEA ) . <p> This region is home to the vast majority of humanity -- where billions are gaining access to reliable electricity for the first time . <p> Belief in climate change is not required to see the strategic and economic significance of China 's approach -- or the risk of ideological opposition to renewable development in countries choosing a different path . <h> Reports <h> Cookies on the bne IntelliNews website <p> This site uses cookies - small text files that are placed on your machine to help the site provide a better user experience . @@128045668 <p> The fossil fuels and climate debate has been a dominant one in the small South American country since US energy giant ExxonMobil struck oil off its coast a decade ago . Back then , Guyana was considered a climate champion due to its conservation of lush Amazon forests and strong environmental protection laws , but ExxonMobil 's discovery set the country on a very different path . <p> While the Guyanese government argues that oil extraction and environmental protection can coexist and it has a right as a developing nation to utilise its resources , critics believe that , amid a worsening global climate crisis , Guyana is backing the wrong horse . <p> Add to that what is widely recognised as a poorly negotiated contract with ExxonMobil and its partners , Guyana 's vulnerability to rising sea levels , and rapid inflation , and you have a complex landscape that threatens the country 's stability and future prosperity . <p> Still , the oil has been flowing since 2019 at a rate unprecedented in the industry . " When the fifth project comes online next year , we @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ 10 years , " says Alistair Routledge , president of ExxonMobil Guyana . " For most other basins , it takes 50 years . " <p> This speed of extraction concerns environmentalists , who have lobbied the government to hold ExxonMobil to unlimited liability in the event of an oil spill ( ExxonMobil has posted a $2 billion guarantee while it appeals a court ruling on the issue ) -- a political football still being kicked around in advance of the country 's general elections in September . <p> All this makes the recent ICJ opinion stating that countries are legally liable for the actions of the private sector all the more relevant . <p> While Routledge says ExxonMobil is " committed to delivering what society is looking for and ensuring we 're doing that in a way that protects the environment " , many take assurances such as these as empty promises . <p> Melinda Janki , a Guyanese international lawyer who was instrumental in persuading her government to include the right to a healthy environment as a fundamental constitutional right in the early 2000s , says that renewables @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ . <p> That is , Janki says , despite IMF figures showing governments subsidised fossil fuels to the tune of $7 trillion in 2022 alone . " <p> Mike McCormack , a representative of the Guyana Extractive Industries Transparency Initiative , echoes that view , dismissing the notion that Guyana has a right to use its oil to catch up with the rest of the world . <p> " The problem is the way you are trying to catch up is the same reason you 're behind now , " he says . @@128045701 <p> Climate scientists have criticised Ireland over proposed measures that would weaken the country 's commitments to reducing methane emissions . <p> Countries that export livestock -- including Ireland and New Zealand -- have proposed a new approach to climate targets , known as " temperature neutrality " . <p> Scientists say this method would undermine efforts to create a sustainable global food system as it allows countries to maintain high agricultural methane emissions while claiming to meet climate targets . <p> A study containing the warning , published in Environmental Research Letters today , was led by University of Galway researchers with input from scientists at University College Cork ( UCC ) , University of Melbourne , and Climate Resource , which models climate scenarios . <p> " If every country adopted a temperature neutrality target , we 'd seriously jeopardise the Paris Agreement 's goal of limiting warming to 1.5 degrees or even 2 degrees , " said lead researcher Dr Colm Duffy , a lecturer in agri-sustainability at University of Galway . <p> " Worse still , this approach does n't just weaken climate ambition , it @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ the status quo for wealthy countries while placing an unfair burden on poorer , food-insecure countries , limiting their ability to grow their own food systems . " <p> This approach , the research concludes , " dramatically reduces the level of ambition needed for overall greenhouse-gas emission reduction " . <p> Revised targets using the new method were proposed to the Government by the Climate Change Advisory Council , an independent body , in part to reduce potential disruption from Ireland 's legal commitment to achieve national " climate neutrality " by 2050 . <p> Minister for Climate and Energy Darragh O'Brien has told the Oireachtas climate committee he has " not formed a definitive view on that one way or the other " . All views from experts , " who will have different views on this " , will be assessed in determining carbon budgets later this year , he said . <p> Temperature neutrality is based on stabilising a country 's contribution to global warming rather than aiming for the more demanding existing target of net zero emissions . <p> In effect , the researchers say @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ , and denying methane emission " rights " to countries producing less of the gas . <p> As methane emissions are strongly linked with agricultural production , they say widespread adoption of temperature neutrality would lock in current inequalities in the global food system by reducing the need to curtail or offset methane emissions by current major livestock exporters . <p> The study shows " temperature neutrality severely restricts development space for agriculture in low-income , food-insecure countries where livestock products are most needed to improve nutrition " . <p> The researchers evaluated scenarios to assess impact from the policy -- first proposed in New Zealand -- on global mean temperatures . Apart from " business as usual " , the temperature neutrality approach performed the worst in terms of global warming emissions . <p> By 2050 , Ireland 's per capita methane emissions would remain almost six times the global average , it finds . <p> Dr Duffy added : " The science shows the new policy essentially grandfathers methane emissions -- meaning a country 's future share of warming is based not on equity or ambition , but @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ I had more , so I get more ' . " <p> UCC sustainable energy specialist Prof Hannah Daly said : " Methane emissions are responsible for around 40 per cent of global warming ... its short lifetime in the atmosphere means that cutting its emissions is an essential lever to limit global warming to safe levels . <p> " For a country like Ireland , with outsized methane emissions , to base our long-term climate target on simply stabilising warming is inadequate to meet our global obligations and sets a dangerous precedent . " <p> Dr R ? is ? n Moriarty of UCC Sustainability Institute said : " A ' no additional warming ' approach to target-setting amounts to backsliding on a country 's commitment to the Paris Agreement and is not a reflection of ' highest possible ambition ' . @@128086572 <h> How methane is regarded in policy terms has big implications for carbon budgets up to 2040 , which must be signed off on soon <p> The Government faces key choices on how to treat methane emissions in its climate policy . Photograph : Alan Betson <p> Wed Aug 13 2025 - 20:33 <p> Methane arising from agriculture and the fossil fuel industry is a notorious greenhouse gas , contributing significantly to global warming . While it breaks down relatively quickly in the atmosphere -- unlike carbon dioxide -- it is a serious threat to climate stabilisation . The quicker it is reduced , the more global average temperatures can be cut . <p> Much can be done to reduce methane releases in oil and gas production , but it is much more challenging in agriculture . Livestock exporting countries like Ireland and New Zealand are supporting a new approach to classifying methane which is gaining political traction . This is based around achieving national " temperature neutrality " , also known as " no additional warming " . <p> The Government has yet to take a formal position on @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ Climate Change Advisory Council , its key advisory body . It chose to interpret Ireland 's legally-binding climate neutrality obligations in terms of temperature neutrality -- rather than the more onerous target of " net zero emissions " . <p> An international study , published in the Environmental Research Letters journal this week , has questioned this approach . It says that it " grandfathers high emissions from wealthy , livestock-exporting nations " , shifts the burden of cutting emissions to others , and limits space for lower-income countries to grow food systems . The approach fails on food security grounds , with trade data showing most exports serve high-income markets . This risks locking in inequality and misses " a critical opportunity to reduce peak warming " . <p> The most concerning finding was highlighted by lead researcher Dr Colm Duffy of the University of Galway : " If every country adopted a temperature neutrality target , we 'd seriously jeopardise the Paris agreement 's goal of limiting warming to 1.5 degrees or even 2 degrees . " <p> The Government is in a bind on this . How @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ carbon budgets up to 2040 , which must be signed off on soon . <p> Climatologist Prof John Sweeney has argued that the shift , which reduces the required emissions reduction target , is not aligned with scientific recommendations and could hinder Ireland 's ability to meet its climate goals . The study emphasises that governments must set targets that are internationally credible and farmers need to be supported within a national framework , rather than targeted for blame . <p> The bottom line is that Ireland will not meet the global climate challenge by redefining climate targets . Ireland has yet to deliver an honest appraisal of what a genuinely climate-neutral , sustainable and resilient agricultural sector could look like in coming decades . Avoiding this issue would amount to backsliding on vital climate action .

@@128086904 <p> In February 2021 , Texas came within four minutes and 37 seconds of a complete statewide blackout . A winter storm knocked out 40 percent of the state 's grid capacity in just four hours , leaving millions without heat or power in sub-zero temperatures . It was a vivid warning : America 's electric grid is aging , overstressed , and dangerously unprepared for the demands of the 21st century . <p> Those demands are growing fast . The US is seeing a steady three percent year-on-year rise in electricity consumption , driven by everything from electric vehicles and heat pumps to AI-powered data centers whose energy appetites have spiked local demand . <p> Meanwhile , renewable energy is reshaping how and when electricity flows , flooding the grid with solar power at midday , then dropping off sharply at night in the now-infamous " duck curve . " <p> This is the new reality : higher demand , more volatile supply , and a grid built for neither . Most of America 's transmission and distribution network dates back decades , with many transformers and substations older @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ of Civil Engineers recently gave the nation 's energy infrastructure a grade of D+ . <p> The fix wo n't be cheap . Replacing the entire US grid would cost nearly $5 trillion . Even targeted modernization will demand unprecedented investment , over $20 billion from the Department of Energy , $73 billion from the Bipartisan Infrastructure Law , and a projected $1.1 trillion in private utility spending between 2025 and 2029 . <p> In Arizona , engineers are installing a new high-voltage DC ( HVDC ) line that will shuttle solar power across nearly 550 miles ( 885 kilometers ) . In New York , an AI system is being developed for an outage prediction model . And across the Midwest , transformers older than disco are finally being replaced . <p> This is the largest power infrastructure transformation in US history . But what does that scale of change actually look like up close ? Under the hood , it 's an engineering challenge spanning HVDC superconductors , AI-driven " smart " grids , massive battery farms , and microgrids designed to run entire towns independently when disaster @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ grid and why the stakes could n't be higher . <h> Why the grid needs an overhaul <p> The American electric grid was n't built for the world we live in today . <p> Much of it took shape in the post -- World War II boom , designed around a simple model : a handful of giant coal and nuclear plants generating steady , predictable electricity that flowed in one direction , from the power station to the customer . It was stable , controllable , and slow to change . <p> That design no longer works . Renewable generation is clean but unpredictable . Solar panels flood the grid with power on sunny afternoons , only to vanish from the supply curve when the sun goes down . Wind power ebbs and surges with the weather . The result is a daily balancing act , ramping other generators up and down at high speed , that the original grid was never engineered to handle . <p> This model is fundamentally incompatible with today 's energy landscape . <h> The forces reshaping demand <p> Electricity demand is surging from @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ new charging points to the grid . Heat pumps are replacing gas furnaces in homes across the country . Data centers powering AI applications are consuming unprecedented amounts of electricity -- driving up power demand by 160 percent in some areas . <p> Meanwhile , renewable energy creates new complexity . Solar panels generate massive amounts of electricity when the sun shines , creating surges that can overwhelm the grid during peak production hours . <p> Climate change has turned weather from a manageable risk into an existential threat . The 2021 Texas winter storm revealed just how vulnerable our infrastructure has become . 40 percent of the grid 's capacity went offline in four hours , with the system just 4 minutes and 37 seconds from a complete statewide blackout . <p> Private utilities are stepping up as well . According to the Edison Electric Institute 's 2024 Financial Review , investor-owned electric companies invested a record $178.2 billion in 2023 -- the 13th consecutive year of record-setting grid spending . These companies project another $1.1 trillion in investment between 2025 and 2029 . <p> The scale of investment @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ kind of grid that can handle these new challenges while remaining reliable 24/7 . <h> Engineering the transition <p> So where exactly is all this funding going ? The answer lies in deploying modern engineering solutions designed to handle challenges the original grid was never built for . <h> HVDC <p> HVDC transmission represents one of the most significant advances in power delivery . Unlike traditional AC lines , HVDC can carry electricity across vast distances with up to 50% fewer losses over long distances . <p> This matters enormously for renewable energy . <p> Wind farms in New Mexico can now efficiently send power to cities in Arizona through projects like SunZia , which carries wind power 550 miles between the two states . Solar installations can also provide electricity to population centers located hundreds of miles away . <p> According to the Department of Energy , HVDC transmission could play a critical role in developing a least-cost , reliable , carbon-free grid . <p> The challenge ? Currently , only five HVDC lines exist in the United States , with expensive converter stations creating a breakeven point at @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ intelligence and storage <p> Think of smart grids as giving the power system a nervous system -- digital sensors and AI algorithms that monitor electricity flow in real time , predict when demand will spike , and spot equipment failures before they cause blackouts . <p> As mentioned , in New York , utilities are already testing AI models that can anticipate grid failures before they happen . It 's predictive maintenance on a massive scale . Then there 's storage -- the grid 's new memory bank . <p> Massive battery facilities , such as California 's Moss Landing , absorb excess wind and solar power when generation is high and release it during peak demand hours . <p> However , this technology is accompanied by various challenges . In early 2025 , a significant fire at Moss Landing emphasized that safety and emergency systems must keep pace with the rapid growth of storage capacity nationwide . <h> Microgrids <p> Microgrids represent the ultimate grid flexibility -- localized networks that operate independently during outages while connecting normally to the main grid . <p> These systems deliver dependable power by @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ technologies , even when the primary grid is down . <p> Deployment of microgrid systems is rapidly expanding , especially in disaster-prone areas and critical facilities such as hospitals . <p> The engineering challenge lies in achieving a seamless transition between grid-connected and islanded modes while preserving power quality throughout the process . <h> The reality check <p> The reality of building a trillion-dollar US grid goes far beyond just investments into several problems . <h> Supply chain crisis <p> Over the past few years , transformer lead times have exploded from around 50 weeks in 2021 to 120 weeks on average in 2024 . Large power transformers now face lead times ranging from 80 to 210 weeks , with some utilities confronting nearly four-year waits . <h> Interconnection nightmare <p> The Lawrence Berkeley National Laboratory reports that a staggering 2,600GW of generating capacity is sitting idle in interconnection queues nationwide , more than double America 's current installed fleet of 1,279GW . <p> The average project built in 2023 took almost five years from requesting interconnection to beginning operations , up from three years in 2015 . About 80 @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ and costs . <p> This matters because grid modernization has become central to energy sovereignty . Mastery of renewable integration and smart grid innovation will allow nations to lead the next phase of economic growth . <p> However , rewiring the grid is n't just engineering -- it 's civilizational . The choices we make now about transmission protocols , energy storage solutions , and cybersecurity will shape the future of energy for generations . <p> 0COMMENT <h> ABOUT THE AUTHOR <p> Tejasri Gururaj Tejasri is a versatile freelance science writer and journalist dedicated to making complex research accessible and engaging for all . She earned her Master 's in Physics from NIT Karnataka , giving her a strong foundation for translating intricate scientific concepts into accessible stories for everyone . <p> NEWSLETTER <h> The Blueprint Daily <p> Stay up-to-date on engineering , tech , space , and science news with The Blueprint .

@@128087616 <h> How to read the news <p> Q : I seem to be reading only bad news in most of the newspapers and online . Am I just not noticing the good news stories or are they not even being published ( or happening ! ) ? <p> A : When it comes to the climate -- and a lot of other things going on in the world -- it does indeed seem as if we 're always reading negative news . The positive stories we come upon , like species , lands , or seas being successfully protected , tend to feel surprising and few and far between , and it 's sometimes difficult to know what to believe . <p> We might very well be overwhelmed by the onslaught , but , according to psychology researcher Afroditi A. Asimakopoulou , it 's actually much more complicated , with several factors at play , including the positive and negative information or misinformation we get in the news , whether we believe it or not , and our own prior emotional state . There are also open questions about just @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ for the good . <p> Asimakopoulou , who is about to complete her doctorate at the School of Psychology at University College Dublin , knows from her own research that the mood with which we come to the news matters . She studies how emotional factors affect our susceptibility to fake news , testing the theory that people who are in a positive or negative emotional state " tend to believe and remember information that is congruent with their emotional state , " she explains . " That if I am in a positive emotional state , I will pay attention more to the positive news , it will stick to my mind more , versus the negative or neutral . " And , whether the " emotionally-valenced " would be remembered more than the neutral . <p> NEUTRAL NEWS <p> She discovered this theory to be true only to a point . From a series of experiments , she found " people tend to believe the negative information , the negative news headline , " perhaps because the negative sticks with us more because of its emotional content , @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ moment , or that what 's negative seems the most possible , but these explanations have not yet been shown in experiments . <p> They also found that people often created " false memories , " or " memories we have about events and information that has never happened before or it has happened a bit differently , " especially for neutral news . In other words , in the face of neutral news , you might not have seen something before but could claim that you have . <p> Asimakopoulou and her fellow researchers expected false memories to factor in more often for positive or negative news , because of the connection to more emotional content , but " it was more , when the information does n't have emotional content , it 's just information on its own , people can say , ' actually , I have seen that , ' and there is no emotional valence attached . " <p> Although not statistically significant , the trend seen in their research was that " people who were in the positive emotional state also imported more false @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ our memory is malleable , she explains , " we try to put together pieces from different parts of the brain , and we remember and forget certain pieces . " <p> SCEPTICISM CAN BE HELPFUL <p> And while we might tend to be more believing when our outlooks are more positive , there is some benefit to your bad mood : we are often more sceptical when we come to the news with a negative mindset . We are more critical and better able to question things . <p> " It has been found before that people in a negative mood tend to remember more and be better at thinking and discriminating between real and fake information . " On the flip side , those in a more positive mood , " are more able to accept things . " <p> Along with that , one important finding , in agreement with past research , is that , whatever your mood , more analytical , critical thinkers are able to take in the news with more scepticism , and scepticism can be helpful . <p> " People who had @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ analytical thinkers , were less susceptible to fake news and able to identify which news was fake and which was true . " <p> What also plays a powerful role , Asimakopoulou explains , are your preexisting beliefs . When it comes to climate change , " I will probably believe the misinformation aligned with my preexisting belief , " which means we have to pay careful attention to what we 're reading and how we 're reading it . " If I am vegan , for example , I will read positive or negative views on veganism in a certain way . " <p> They also found , in particular with climate change , one-off exposure to fake news does not tend to change behaviours or impact how you read the news otherwise . <p> CHECK YOUR SOURCES <p> This might all be well and good , but cultivating a negative mood hardly seems the healthiest way to be a savvier digester of the news . Better , Asimakopoulou suggests , is to get your news from multiple , varied sources , like newspapers , radio , podcasts , @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ In other words : check your sources , even sources you 've long counted on and trust , as they can change . <p> And as for your critical thinking , these are skills that can be cultivated : one tip is to always question your preexisting beliefs , or what you might already think you know . These are beliefs which might lead to a bias . And even getting into the habit of checking your sources will make yours a more critical mind . <p> So , the good news about your bad mood is it might make you a better interpreter of the news . But , no matter how you 're feeling , getting your news from a wider range of outlets , looking to primary sources , and working on your skills at critical thinking will make you the best possible interpreter of all the news you encounter , good and bad . <p> This website uses cookies to improve your experience . @@128127556 <p> I launched into telling my kids about video rental shops : places where families argued in public aisles , vied for VHS tapes , and paid fines as a rite of passage . <p> 1 . Friday night was an event <p> When I told my kids that movie night used to mean leaving the house , hunting through aisles , and negotiating a family truce over which VHS to take home -- rather than just scrolling and putting Shrek 2 on again -- they looked at me as though I 'd confessed to eating gravel daily . <p> In the 90s , Friday night meant one thing : a pilgrimage to Xtra-vision or Chartbusters . You did n't " pick a film " -- you committed to an evening-long family summit , complete with heated debates in the aisles . <p> Back then , choice was both a blessing and a curse . <p> There were no algorithms spoon-feeding you , " Because you watched Mrs. Doubtfire , here 's every film Robin Williams ever made . " <p> You had to walk the aisles yourself , judging @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ one copy was n't already gone . <p> ( And when it was gone , oh , the heartbreak . That empty space behind the display box ? That was my first experience of grief . ) <p> Video rental shops sprang up throughout Ireland during the 1980s , thanks to the advent of the VCR . <p> By the early 90s , nearly every town had one . Xtra-vision alone had over 200 stores nationwide at its peak . <p> If you were under 18 or over 40 and wanted to stay in on a Friday night , this was your spot . <p> The former Xtravision , Douglas , Cork : one of many houses of wonder that used to dot the high streets of Ireland <p> 2 . Late fees were real and personal <p> My children have never known true fear . <p> I 'm not talking about the modern fears -- climate change , AI , or the Wi-Fi going down . <p> I mean the creeping dread of realising at 8.55pm that your rented movie was due back at 6pm ... three days ago @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ -- the late fee was more than I could have invested as a junior producer . <p> But the real cost was the shame : the staff would scan your card , look you in the eye , and calmly announce the fee to the whole shop . Culturally , this was Ireland at its best : a system based on guilt . <p> The staff did n't have to say anything . They 'd just scan your card , pause , look at you over the top of the computer , and quietly announce the fee . The whole shop heard it . <p> Suddenly , you were n't just the fella renting Speed 2 : Cruise Control -- you were the community 's delinquent . <p> 3 . @@128128043 <h> No amount of wishing or hoping for an imaginary carbon unicorn will make it true <p> As the climate crisis worsens and inches closer to a global catastrophe , the world is desperate to find solutions and quick fixes . Climate catastrophe is no longer a distant threat , it is our present reality . <p> It is manifesting as a litany of disasters from destructive wildfires reducing homes and forests to ash , to devastating storms and floods washing away livelihoods and claiming lives . <p> It 's easy to understand the desperation for quick-fix solutions . <p> Carbon market mechanisms have long been the favourite climate " solution " among parts of the private sector in the Global North , and some governments . This is where countries or companies buy " carbon credits " from countries or communities in the Global South to offset their emissions . It sounds appealing , and those in favour argue countries or companies unable to cut their emissions in the short term can fund climate action for those bearing the brunt of the crisis . <h> READ MORE <p> However @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ rosy picture . <p> Carbon market projects almost always fail to provide any real climate benefit . Worse , they have a long history of subjecting host communities to various abuses and human rights violations . They simply do not work . And even if they could provide some relief , the idea that the benefit would somehow make ongoing emissions elsewhere acceptable as the world careers towards nearly 3 degrees of warming is indefensible . <p> Multiple investigations have shown most carbon markets do not represent real emission reductions . A study into Verra , a leading crediting programme , found more than 90 per cent of its rainforest offsets were " phantom credits " -- in other words , worthless for the climate . <p> Another review by Corporate Accountability and the Guardian found 39 of the top 50 projects were probably " junk " . A 2024 Nature Communications study , covering about a fifth of all issued credits , found that less than 16 per cent of those credits represented real reductions in emissions . <p> Even when carbon markets deliver the promised climate action , @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ an offset . For example , a major oil company or petrostate might claim " net zero " progress by funding a tree-planting initiative in Kenya . But while a few thousand trees are planted in the Global South , oil drilling and fossil fuel expansion continue in the Global North . The emissions are n't reduced , they 're just offset on paper . The climate still loses , but the polluter gets to look green . <p> Some climate action may be happening but it is in the context of emissions being allowed elsewhere -- just greenwashed to look acceptable . <p> While many projects claim they will bring benefits to a region , including jobs or other investments , they not only have a history of failing to deliver for communities , but also have a record of violence , displacement , abuse and other injustices . <p> A Carbon Brief investigation has found more than 100 documented cases of harm to host communities over the past five years . Communities are displaced from their land or lose access to forest in the name of stopping deforestation @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ of the deforestation . <p> Recently published ActionAid USA research , Caution Required : Protecting Communities from Carbon Markets , focuses on Kenya , which has the largest and oldest carbon projects in Africa . Many of these illustrate the problems with exaggeration of emissions avoided , carbon sequestered and uncertainty of carbon credit value as well as risks to communities . <p> Of the 317 carbon projects registered with voluntary carbon markets in Kenya , most are small cookstoves or water-filtration projects . While they may offer health benefits , studies show these projects often exaggerate climate impact by overstating the uptake of cookstoves and water filters -- and firewood saved . Private companies and organisations that implement these projects and purchase the credits typically benefit far more than the communities . <p> Most of Kenya 's largest carbon projects involve attempts to alter land use and control . The largest carbon credit project is the Kasigau Wildlife Corridor Project , run by Wildlife Works , a company originating in the United States . The project claims to avoid carbon emissions by protecting against deforestation , but there are @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ and community marginalisation . <p> An investigation by the Kenyan Human Rights Commission and the Dutch organisation SOMO found there was a significant pattern of sexual harassment and abuse by senior Wildlife Works employees and rangers of local women , female employees and the wives of junior male workers . <p> The overall experience of projects in Kenya was that they lacked legitimate , informed and prior consultation with affected communities . They were not consistently established with regard to community land rights laws and UN land tenure guidelines . Additionally , " middlemen " and technical consultants took much of the economic benefit before it reached communities . <p> Unfortunately , this dismal record in the Global South has not deterred interest in carbon markets or carbon credits . Buyers -- typically corporations or governments under pressure to reduce emissions -- are generally looking to purchase credits for carbon offsets . On the other hand , sellers -- often poorer Global South governments or local communities -- are generally not motivated by supposed climate benefits , but rather by the need for revenue . <p> Most experts agree the @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ longer enough time to decarbonise and reduce emissions before crossing the line . Every fraction of a degree of warming worsens impacts , increases devastation , and risks tipping points that collapse ecosystems and drive more climate change . Current policies put the world on track for a near three-degree rise , which would be catastrophic . Yet instead of urgent systemic change , carbon offsets offer a false solution , letting polluters claim progress without cutting emissions at source . <p> Carbon markets persist due to the failure to date to provide adequate and quality climate finance , a key pillar of the United Nations Framework Convention on Climate Change and the Paris Agreement . Far too little money is flowing . <p> Developed countries promised $100 billion a year in climate finance by 2020 as a good-faith downpayment on what everyone acknowledged was a far greater need . That money was not only late , but what was provided was overwhelmingly in the form of loans , further ratcheting up debt , and double-counted development assistance . <p> When setting the new climate finance target at Cop29 in @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ discussion of the scale of the need for climate finance or have a conversation about the quality of that finance . The resulting goal was a profound disappointment , with vague references to blended finance and more ambition through the private sector . This has deeply strained climate negotiations . <p> ActionAid is calling for an end to offsets as a climate strategy . Carbon markets are not climate finance and do not lessen the climate finance obligations of rich countries , responsible for 92 per cent of carbon dioxide emissions . <p> Instead of false promises , we need real solutions : direct emissions cut at source , progressive taxation on polluters , public climate finance that supports just transitions , and investments in renewable energy and adaptation . Climate justice means putting people and the planet before profit . <p> Communities are being led towards carbon markets to make up for the lack of real climate finance , all while climate impacts are increasing and climate action is becoming more urgent . Therefore , carbon markets represent a policy failure and a moral failure . They present a @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ deserve real support , and governments need the resources to provide public services , not false promises built on continued pollution . <p> For those engaged in carbon markets , governments must ensure land rights and land tenure are protected and not given away as part of any carbon market project . Legal safeguards need to ensure communities are not left holding the bag for failed credits . <p> In the midst of a climate crisis that is wreaking havoc on the planet , the science is clear : there is no room for offsets in a 1.5-degree world . No amount of wishing or hoping for an imaginary carbon unicorn will make it true . <p> Rapid decarbonisation and zero deforestation is the only way to not overshoot the carbon budget . And a world in overshoot is not one we want to see . 
@@128128278 <p> Hydrogen is becoming a cornerstone in the push for cleaner energy . From steelmaking to power generation , industries are exploring hydrogen as a way to cut carbon emissions . <p> Water electrolysis , splitting water into hydrogen and oxygen using electricity , offers a sustainable production method , especially when powered by renewable energy . <p> But most systems still rely on freshwater , a resource under mounting stress due to climate change , population growth , and competing demands from agriculture and industry . <p> Seawater electrolysis could help bypass this limitation , but the technology faces serious hurdles , from corrosion caused by chloride ions to performance loss under industrial-scale operating conditions . <p> Now , researchers in South Korea say they have taken a significant step toward solving those problems . <p> Dr. Ji-Hyung Han 's team at the Korea Institute of Energy Research ( KIER ) has developed a carbon cloth-based electrode that runs stably for more than 800 hours under high current while directly using seawater . <h> Tackling corrosion and performance loss <p> In seawater electrolysis , the electrode support plays a @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ quickly in the presence of chloride ions . Carbon cloth has emerged as an alternative because of its conductivity , corrosion resistance , flexibility , and cost benefits . <p> Yet , existing carbon cloth electrodes have struggled to withstand high current operation above 500 mA/cm ? for more than 100 hours , a basic threshold for industrial use . <p> Han 's team overcame these limitations with an optimized acid treatment process . They immersed the cloth in concentrated nitric acid at 100 ? C for one hour . <p> To prevent acid concentration from fluctuating due to evaporation , they designed a sealed treatment vessel , ensuring stable conditions . <p> Even with only 1% ruthenium by weight , the electrode reduced overpotential by about 25% compared to conventional cobalt-molybdenum catalysts . <p> This translated into a roughly 1.3 times more efficient hydrogen evolution reaction at the same current density . <p> The electrode maintained its initial performance after more than 800 hours of continuous operation at 500 mA/cm ? . <p> Tests revealed no leaching of ruthenium or cobalt into the electrolyte , underscoring the material 's @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ produced a 25 cm ? version of the electrode , suggesting scalability for industrial modules . <p> " This technology marks the world 's first successful case of long-term operation over one month under industrial-level high current conditions in seawater electrolysis using a carbon cloth-based electrode , " Han said . <p> She added , " We plan to further advance the technology to the demonstration level through extended durability testing beyond 1,000 hours and research on scaling up to large-area cell modules and stacks . @@128128285 <p> At Michigan State University 's Pollinator Performance Center , researchers are enlisting an unusual ally in their efforts to protect honeybees from a deadly bacterial disease , a nine-year-old English springer spaniel named Maple . <p> Formerly a human remains detection dog for the St. Joseph County sheriff 's office , Maple is now trained to detect American foulbrood , a bacterial infection that kills honeybee larvae and can devastate entire hives . The disease has been one of several factors , alongside insecticides , climate change , and reduced food diversity , contributing to the decline of bees and other pollinators in recent years . <p> American foulbrood is a costly problem for beekeepers . Once a hive is infected , it can lead to the death of young bees and force keepers to burn honey crops and equipment to stop the spread . " Beekeepers ... have had to burn tens of thousands of dollars of equipment due to this disease , " said Meghan Milbrath , an assistant professor in MSU 's entomology department , in an interview with WILX . <h> From K-9 service to @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ career change came after she suffered an on-the-job injury in Ontario , prompting her retirement from police work in 2024 . Her longtime owner , trainer , and handler , Sue Stejskal , a Michigan State graduate with over 25 years of experience training dogs for law enforcement and other uses , was looking for a new way to channel Maple 's high energy . <p> Around the same time , Milbrath was working to develop diagnostic and screening tools for honeybee diseases . A veterinarian who had attended a honeybee training session introduced Milbrath to Stejskal , and the idea of using canine scent detection in beekeeping quickly took shape . <p> Stejskal set about retraining Maple to identify the scent markers of American foulbrood inside beehives , adapting methods used in police detection work . Maryland 's agriculture department has also used dogs for similar purposes , but Michigan State 's goal is to expand the approach and train many more dogs to join the effort . <h> Working in the field <p> Because Maple works directly in active bee yards , she wears specialized protective gear , @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ to shield her paws . " Much like with humans , we recognize that if a dog is going to be in an active bee yard , they need to wear the same personal protective equipment as people , " Stejskal said . " You ca n't buy them on Amazon for dogs . So , there 's been some altering and testing . " <p> During a recent demonstration , Maple darted between hive boxes , stopping at the one containing a hidden scent clue . When she located the source , she calmly looked to Stejskal , who praised her with , " Good girl . Yes , " and rewarded her with a green , Michigan State-branded toy . <h> RECOMMENDED ARTICLES <p> The Pollinator Performance Center plans to document Maple 's training process and publish a guidebook by Milbrath and Stejskal to help other teams adopt the technique . <p> For Stejskal , the work is about more than science . " It 's a cool project , " she said . " But I was over-the-moon excited , because my dog would still have joy @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ , " she said in her interview with AP . <p> With about 465 bee species native to Michigan alone , researchers say every effort to reduce disease risks is vital . Maple 's new role demonstrates how specialized detection skills can be adapted to protect critical pollinators , and how a working dog 's career can take a second life , for the benefit of both bees and beekeepers . <p> 0COMMENT <h> ABOUT THE AUTHOR <p> Kaif Shaikh Kaif Shaikh is a journalist and writer passionate about turning complex information into clear , impactful stories . @@128146093 <h> Growing Pains : Challenges And Opportunities In Vertical Farming <p> August 14 , 2025 8:00 AM <p> By Jimmy Nicholls <p> Share this article <p> Share this article <p> Producing food in a controlled environment with the help of robots sounds like the perfect way to assure supplies . So why have some vertical farms struggled , while others are expanding . Jimmy Nicholls reports . This article first appeared in ESM 's July/August 2025 edition . <p> There is something distinctly science fiction about vertical farms . Sprawling across greenhouses and tucked into city nooks , plants are stacked , layer upon layer -- their roots soaking in a nutrient solution or sprayed with a mist . <p> Robots are left to do much of the tending , with human specialists tinkering in search of higher yields . It 's a concept that is only becoming more attractive , as climate change makes traditional farming increasingly unreliable . <p> Within the past year , cooler spring temperatures in the Netherlands have led to slower growth for tomatoes , cucumbers and lettuce , while in the summer , late @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ . <p> Last spring , Europe experienced an exceptionally dry season , according to the European Environment Agency , during which 37 of the 38 countries that it covers were hit by drought -- and that 's just Europe . <p> ADVERTISEMENT <p> At its best , controlled-environment agriculture can mitigate against increasingly chaotic weather , according to the technology 's advocates . <p> There certainly have been success stories , but these victories have been marred by a slew of start-ups that have fallen into difficulties as they search for the right commercial model . <p> In May , Jones Food Company became the latest casualty in the industry , being taken into administration as it laid off 61 employees . <p> The company was once the largest vertical-farming company in the UK , and one of the biggest in the world , with a total of 20,000 square metres of grow space at its two farms . <p> ADVERTISEMENT <p> Such is the scepticism of prospective investors that even early Jones backer Ocado has indicated it will not increase its shareholding , making it more likely that Jones @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ as a casualty of vertical-farming entrepreneurship , providing more fodder for critics who contend that investors are enchanted by the technology , but negligent about commercial viability . <p> " Jones Food Company survived for a relatively long time and was backed by knowledgeable investors and managers , " says Cindy van Rijswick , a global strategist at Rabobank . " That shows how difficult it is to have a commercially viable vertical farm . " <p> Certainly , the critics have a point , but look beyond Europe and to the latest technological innovations , and there are signs that collapses are just growing pains before a sustainable sector takes root . <h> Counting The Costs <p> Industry observers can at least agree on one thing : vertical farming is complex and costly . Growing crops outside of soil requires ingenious thinking . <p> ADVERTISEMENT <p> For some farmers , it means submerging plant roots in nutrient-rich liquid ( hydroponics ) , while some suspend the roots in air and spray them with a similar solution ( aeroponics ) . <p> Others take fish waste and use it as @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ mostly removes the need for soil . As for sunlight , that has to be replaced with LED lights tailored to plants ' different growth stages . <p> Farmers also control temperature , humidity , CO2 levels and airflow , employing large amounts of automation and robotics to tend to the vegetables . <p> " Capital investment for infrastructure is substantial , as state-of-the-art , fully automated farms require heavy initial funding , " says Daniele Benatoff , chief executive and co-founder of Planet Farms , an Italian vertical-farming company that is expanding into the UK , but he argues that these investments can pay off in the long term if cleverly managed . <p> ADVERTISEMENT <p> " Technology costs , including automation , can be high up front , but they 're crucial for optimising yields , reducing labour dependency over time , and ensuring quality . To be commercially viable , vertical farms must strike a balance between high-tech efficiency and cost control , often relying on scale , innovation , and strategic partnerships . " <p> It 's not just the price of entry that is high @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ a particular problem in Europe , given the fluctuations in energy prices since the pandemic and Russia 's invasion of Ukraine in 2022 . <p> These energy costs can comprise 30- 60% of the total operational costs for a vertical farm , according to Sidhartha Bhandari , a senior client partner at Publicis Sapient , a consulting firm . <p> " The core challenge in vertical farming is n't the technology -- it 's the economics , " Bhandari says , pointing to labour and maintenance costs , in addition to those of energy . <p> Intelligent Growth Solutions ( IGS ) , a vertical-farming technology provider headquartered in Scotland , has sent its first major consignment of specialist parts to the UAE , for a farm at Dubai Food Tech Valley . <p> This infrastructure project is being developed by Dubai-based group ReFarm Global , as part of an initiative designed to address food security , and will have a growing area of 87,000 square metres . <h> Crop Rotation <p> The other major pressure on vertical farming comes from those crops that can be viably produced and sold @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ focused on leafy greens , herbs and microgreens , owing to the limited space required , the plants ' proven ability to thrive in controlled environments , and the potential for frequent harvests . <p> " Fruits like strawberries are also emerging as viable options , thanks to innovations in lighting and pollination technologies , " Bhandari says . He expects technological progress to further broaden the range of crops grown in vertical farms . <p> Other observers are keen to emphasise the constraints that confine the sector to a small range of vegetables for now . " Some people have tried raspberries , but although the revenue per pound is fairly high , the plants are quite big and bulky , and you can not have as many plants and harvests a year , compared to leafy greens , " says Rabobank 's van Rijswick . <p> This is even more true of root vegetables , such as carrots and potatoes , which are generally considered unsuitable for vertical farming . Challenges include the long growing period -- up to three months for potatoes , and four months for @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ for deep substrates . <p> Van Rijswick believes the focus on niche products , such as microgreens and herbs , prevents the sector from scaling effectively . <p> " To reduce costs per unit of product , most vertical farms aim to increase scale , but this has turned out to be very difficult , for multiple reasons . Sometimes demand is just not sufficient . Sometimes it is just difficult to ramp up production , " she says . <h> Adopting Hybrid Approach <p> This has led some produce companies to adopt a hybrid approach , which uses vertical farms in the early stage of lettuce growth before moving the produce to greenhouses or fields later . <p> This reduces the risk of disease and contamination , while transport costs can be minimised if the different facilities are located on the same site . " We have several clients that have a small vertical farm for the first production phase . I am sure we will see more of these applications of producing starting materials in the future , " van Rijswick says . <p> " For R&amp;D purposes @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ have much more control over the production variables , compared to a greenhouse or field -- and for R&amp;D purposes , the costs are less of an issue . " <h> Success Stories <p> Such factors explain the implosion of several notable vertical-farming companies , but there are still plenty of success stories . Planet Farms , which started life in Italy , is pushing into the British market with the launch of its vertically farmed Fresh Everleaf salad range . <p> Grown without pesticides or herbicides , Fresh Everleaf leaves are untouched until the bag is opened because of Planet Farms ' automated growing system , which also means that the leaves stay fresh in the fridge for at least eight days . <p> The company also claims that 95% less water , 93% less soil and 96% less fertiliser are used in the production process than would be the case with traditional farming . <p> Alongside its Waitrose listing , Planet Farms announced more than ? 25 million of investment in a UK facility in April , equating to 20,000 square meters of growing capacity . <p> Construction @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ to begin in 2027 . The British site will join the company 's first farm -- in Cirimido , Italy -- as well as another facility that Planet Farms is working on in Scandinavia . <p> The development proves that vertical-farm investors are " no longer buying dreams or business plans on paper , but when faced with solid business models , a good track record , and potential for attractive financial returns , they remain interested , " says Planet Farms ' Benatoff . <p> He acknowledges that flourishing firms in vertical farming have become " a small subset of the very large number of startups " in the sector , but this " is not unusual when you look at the history of new technologies " . <p> Planet Farms ' success is corroborated by other expansions in the UK . Last July , GrowUp Farms claimed ? 38 million to expand capacity at its vertical farm in Kent , which already supplies its Unbeleafable salads in Tesco and its Fresh Leaf Co salads in Iceland and SPAR . <p> Part of its success can be attributed to @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ fully powered by renewable energy from the bioenergy plant sited next door . <p> Even so , Jon Close , an engineering partner at Keystone Law , reckons that the case for vertical farming is more compelling in areas outside of Europe . <p> " I 'm seeing more facilities and more UK expertise being deployed overseas than I 'm seeing facilities getting off the ground in the UK . At the moment , the need is outside the UK , " Close says . <p> Vertical-farming companies are also continuing to invest in the Nordic countries . At the start of the year , the Norwegian company Avisomo raised ? 5 million to fund the development of its hydroponic system , with ? 3.4 million coming from previous investors and ? 1.6 million from Innovation Norway , a state-run investment body . <p> The company is running a pilot scheme with Norway 's Coop retailer , with production due to start this summer , with the aim of producing 100 tonnes of fresh lettuce annually . <p> In February , Stockholm-based SweGreen opened its largest farm within a Maxi @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ company specialises in vertical-farming units that sit within supermarkets and operates with ICA franchises and Coop stores in Sweden , as well as the supermarket Edeka , in Germany . <p> " The fact that four major ICA retailers chose to extend their partnerships for up to five years in the fall of 2024 proves that our technology works on a large scale and can be seamlessly integrated into grocery retail . We see strong demand from both retailers and investors who want to be part of the next major shift in the food sector , " says Pierre Mohlin , a partner at SweGreen . <p> At a research centre in Roeselare , Belgium , last September , Urban Crop Solutions commissioned an innovative farming tower , which combines LED lighting with natural sunlight . <p> The company 's chief executive , Jean-Pierre Coene , describes the project as " an example of how technology and nature can work hand in hand towards a sustainable future . " <p> Leafy greens , strawberries and microgreens are the first crops being tested at the installation . Over in the US @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ company SoftBank and the supermarket chain Walmart . <p> The company 's flagship Compton farm is in the centre of Los Angeles , focusing on leafy greens , with a berry farm in Richmond , Virginia , having opened in 2024 . <p> Among the supermarkets that list its products are Whole Foods , Bristol Farms and Misfits Market . <h> Food Security <p> These examples will give some succour to advocates of vertical farming , but many also argue that the sector will become more relevant as climate change continues to make weather patterns more erratic , hampering traditional farming methods and reducing yields . <p> One region that provides strong evidence of this is the Middle East . There , the vertical-farming sector is expected to grow to more than $400 million by 2025 , and nearly double in size by 2030 , according to Mordor Intelligence . <p> Countries including Saudi Arabia and the UAE are all investing heavily in indoor agriculture , as they seek greater food security . " This growth is driven largely by pressing food security concerns , limited arable land , and @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ Saudi Arabia . Additionally , the availability of low-cost and reliable electricity in some Middle Eastern countries makes the economics of vertical farming more attractive to investors , " says Bhandari of Publicis Sapient . <p> " Verticalfarming systems are inherently more resilient to climate-related disruptions , such as droughts , pests , and extreme weather , which makes them a powerful tool for building more robust food systems as global populations grow and environmental pressures intensify . " <p> Combined with government support , Bhandari says that these factors have made vertical farming in the Middle East attractive to investors . " Overall , investor interest in the Middle East is high , propelled by strategic imperatives and a supportive policy environment , " he says . <p> Meanwhile in southeast Asia , Singapore is looking at vertical farming as part of its ambition to grow 30% of its food locally by 2030 -- the so called ' 30 by 30 ' food security initiative . <p> In November , Dutch agritech company Growy opened a vertical farm there , cultivating basil , parsley and kale , and other @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ farming is especially attractive due to the small size of the city-state . <p> Even in larger countries , the technology reduces the need to clear land and cut down forests . Taken alongside industry claims that vertical farming uses much less water , pesticides and fertiliser than traditional farming , it makes for an environmentally friendly packaging -- energy needs notwithstanding . <p> " You can use a lot less water when you can control your growing medium , " says Keystone 's Close . He adds that the ability to create small units in cities can massively reduce food miles , with one of his clients selling urban-grown produce to local restaurants . <p> Such aspects make Close a lot more optimistic about the future of vertical farming than some of the recent stories of bankruptcy might suggest . <p> " The sad demise of Jones Food Company is a setback , but I do n't think it 's a death knell . You 'll find supermarket chains have been trying to do something similar , with varying degrees of success , " he says . <p> Close @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ for them , with some creating large-scale farms and others focusing on smaller urban units . <p> If they can do it with a price point that is competitive with that of conventionally farmed crops , vertical farming has a future .

Other contributing factors included inconsistent track surfaces following unusually heavy rainfall and , in some cases , pressure on trainers to run horses sooner than advisable . <p> In response , more than 60 welfare and safety recommendations were implemented , including : <p> Increased out-of-competition testing and medical record transfer in claiming races . <p> Advanced imaging and biomarker research to detect high-risk horses before breakdowns occur . <p> Weather and surface protocols to avoid high-speed work on compromised ground . <p> The introduction of PET scanning , post-mortem review programmes , and trainer education sessions have , according to Dr Vale , been vital in preventing repeat incidents . " Nutrition can be one of the foundation blocks for preventing a lot of problems and giving our horses the lives they deserve . " <p> Horses , she reminded the audience , are trickle feeders with small stomachs and a digestive system designed for constant intake . Prolonged gaps without forage @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ . " They should be chewing for up to 18 hours a day . At the end of the day , it comes back to good-quality hay . " <p> Core welfare principles - the " three Fs " - were central to her talk : friends , forage and freedom . Horses need social contact , high-fibre diets and regular turnout to maintain both physical and mental health . <p> Among the key points : <p> Ulcers - Affect up to 90% of racehorses in intense training . Best managed with low-starch , low-sugar diets , plenty of forage before exercise , and small , frequent meals . <p> Colic - The leading killer of horses , often linked to dehydration . Adequate electrolytes , especially in hot weather , and access to clean water are vital . <p> O'Connor also highlighted emerging research linking a diverse gut microbiome to improved performance and earnings . " We know foals with more fibre in the diet and fewer antibiotics early in life go on to win more prize money . That 's the power of the microbiome . " <p> @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ talk on training , handling and behaviour , urging the industry to embrace modern science over centuries-old tradition . <p> " The horse 's brain is the same whether we 're talking about a miniature , a Grand Prix show jumper or a racehorse , " she began . " The types of learning are the same - and the science now tells us how to get the best results with less stress for both horse and human . " <p> Doherty argued that advances in genetics , nutrition and surfaces have outpaced progress in behavioural science in the equestrian world . " Training and behaviour knowledge lags way behind , " she warned , " and yet it has a massive impact on safety , welfare and performance . " <p> She emphasised that many so-called " problem " behaviours - napping , jogging , barging - are learned and often unintentionally rewarded . " Horses are n't bold or bad . If a behaviour brings a good outcome , they will repeat it - and that 's how habits @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ she explained , lies in understanding operant conditioning ( associating behaviour with outcome ) and classical conditioning ( associating two events ) . <p> " Pressure and release - what science calls negative reinforcement - is something you all do daily , " she said . " Used correctly , it 's one of the most powerful tools we have . Combine it with rewards , like food or wither scratching , and you not only train the response , you make the horse feel good about doing it . " <p> Dr Doherty cautioned against over-reliance on punishment , noting that delayed or poorly timed corrections often create fear rather than learning . " We should be aiming to produce horses that are calm , compliant and safe to handle - from the mounting block to the veterinary exam . " <p> Her message was as much about mindset as mechanics : " It 's up to us to teach the correct response . " <p> Feed safety under the spotlight at BETA NOPS talk <p> FOR Connolly 's Red Mills ' UK head nutritionist Louise Jones , the focus turned to feed safety and the ever-present risk of prohibited substances in equine diets . Connolly 's Red Mills are a BETA NOPS Working Party member , and Jones highlighted how contamination can occur , how the feed industry works to prevent it , and the steps trainers and owners can take on their own yards . <p> A prohibited substance , the audience was reminded , is any ingredient banned under the relevant sporting authority 's rules . Lists differ slightly between bodies such as the IHRB and FEI , but the consequences of a positive test are the same - loss of prize money , damaged reputation , and potentially costly legal battles . <p> The risk is not theoretical . " It really @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ Just one gram of poppy seeds could trigger a positive for morphine , while as few as 10 peanut M&amp;Ms could register caffeine and theobromine for up to 48 hours . " <p> Some substances occur naturally in raw ingredients , others enter feed via cross-contamination at harvesting , storage , or transport . The 2002 morphine contamination case , and more recently zilpaterol in molasses , were cited as industry-wide wake-up calls that led to tighter controls . <p> Since 2009 , the BETA NOPS Code has provided an independently audited framework for minimising the risk of naturally occurring prohibited substances . Accredited feed companies must monitor their supply chain , audit suppliers , and test both raw materials and finished feeds using forensic-grade equipment . Intelligence on emerging risks is shared across the industry.Trainers and owners can play a crucial role . Jones urged attendees to : <p> Enforce strict yard hygiene : no eating or drinking in feed areas , designated coloured buckets for medicated horses , and awareness of other animals on site . <p> " By choosing the right supplier and tightening best practice on @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ test dramatically . <p> " Feed safety is n't just about the mill -- it 's about everyone in the chain doing their part . " <p> Nutrition at the heart <p> of racehorse recovery <p> The spotlight turned to practical nutrition strategies for post-race recovery , with a clear message : feed with purpose in the 48 hours after exertion . <p> Nutritionist Nicole Groyer of Connolly 's Red Mills outlined the " four Rs " of recovery - replenish glycogen , rehydrate , repair muscle , and re-establish gut health . Carbohydrate stores , especially muscle glycogen , are the main energy source for high-intensity exercise . but can take 24-48 hours to fully restore in horses . " Depletion can reduce anaerobic capacity by up to 60% in subsequent work , " the audience was told . Rest , ad-lib forage , small meals and timely electrolyte supplementation were recommended to speed replenishment . <p> High-quality protein is vital for repairing micro-tears and supporting muscle adaptation . Soybean meal was highlighted as an excellent lysine source , with branched-chain amino acids ( valine , isoleucine , leucine @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ fatigue . Antioxidants such as vitamin E , selenium and CoQ10 help counter oxidative stress , reducing stiffness and inflammation . <p> Rehydration was another priority . " Horses ca n't rehydrate on water alone , " the audience heard . Sweating leads to electrolyte as well as fluid loss , and replacement is critical for muscle contraction , nerve function and digestion . Frequent small drinks once cooled , plus electrolyte supplementation , were advised . Forage plays a dual role , acting " like a sponge " to hold water in the gut . <p> Gut health underpins performance and recovery . Factors such as fasting before racing , high-starch meals , stress and dehydration can lead to ulcers , hindgut acidosis and disrupted microbiota . Groyer recommended constant access to quality forage to create a ' fibre mat ' in the stomach , preventing acid splash during exercise , alongside targeted gut supplements ( pre- , pro- and postbiotics ) where needed . <p> A National Hunt horse prone to ulcers and weight loss - controlled-starch , slow-release energy diet , oil top-up for calories , mash @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ during veterinary treatment . <p> The take-home ? Recovery is an active process . " If you get it wrong , you risk performance loss and injury . Get it right , and you give your horse the best chance to come back stronger , " the presenter concluded . <p> ' A life worth living ' -- welfare lecture calls <p> for 24-hour approach <p> SPEAKING for a second time , Dr Alina Vale praised the work already being done by organisations such as Horse Racing Ireland and the IHRB , but urged the industry to think beyond race day . <p> " It 's the other 23 hours that create the positive outcomes you see on the track , " she stressed . Drawing on the American Association of Equine Practitioners ' ( AAEP ) equine welfare principles , she defined good welfare as horses being healthy , comfortable , well-nourished , safe , able to express natural behaviours and free from pain , fear and distress . <p> The Five Domains model was presented as a framework for reducing avoidable negative experiences and ensuring racehorses enjoy " @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ . <p> Simple management changes , she noted , can have big welfare impacts - from improving bedding depth for REM sleep , to adding windows between boxes for social contact , to ensuring constant access to quality forage . " In too many places , particularly under strict pre-race regimes , feed and water are still removed far too early , " she warned . <p> Turnout was flagged as a particular weakness in some jurisdictions . " In parts of the US , racehorses may never see a grass paddock in their career , " she said , highlighting the need for safe , well-managed turnout as part of everyday welfare . <p> The presentation also addressed aftercare , emphasising the owner 's responsibility to secure a suitable second career or retirement . The speaker voiced concern about the growing number of surgically-repaired horses - such as those with fetlock arthrodesis - and questioned whether enough homes could accommodate their long-term needs . <p> Euthanasia guidelines from the AAEP were reviewed , with the message that humane death is preferable to prolonged suffering or risky long-distance transport for @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ rest/water intervals , were also discussed . Dr Vale challenged attendees to identify one change they could make immediately to improve welfare in their own yard - and to consider which welfare parameters should be part of future assessments <p> Draft IHRB welfare and safety <p> standards shared <p> Lynne Hillyer , chief veterinary officer at IHRB , gave the first public outline of the new Irish Thoroughbred Welfare and Safety Standards . The new Standards come under specific objectives in the IHRB Satement of Strategy welfare pillars , and they are now entering industry consultation . <p> The first notable measure is a clear emphasis on responsible veterinary care . Medicines should be used " judiciously , when they need to be used , " Hillyer said , with veterinary professionals " front and centre " in all decisions . Hillyer stressed the aim is not to restrict necessary treatment , but to ensure it is always carried out legally , appropriately , and with the horse 's long-term welfare in mind . <p> The second proposed change is a stronger duty of care when a horse leaves a @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ to know , and stand over , where the horse is going , who is responsible for its transport , and the conditions in which it will travel . " This might sound ambitious , but it has to happen , " Lynne said , adding that it was " responsible for our industry to make this happen " in light of recent public concerns . The draft standards will now undergo " proper consultation " with the wider industry in the coming months . <p> Dr David Marlin : " Cooling is the one thing that will save your horse - and your sport 's reputation " <p> ON what turned out to be one of the hottest days in Ireland this year , Dr David Marlin , one of the equine world 's leading scientists , delivered a stark and practical message : racing in hot conditions requires more than common sense - it requires science , preparation and urgency . <p> " Whether you believe climate change is new or not , it does n't matter , " Marlin began . " We are having more heatwaves @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ to nine major European heatwaves in the last five years , compared with just three or four in the previous three decades . <p> Horses , he explained , produce heat three to five times faster than humans during exercise . " For every unit of energy a muscle uses to move , four times as much is released as heat . During a race , muscle temperature can rise by one and a half degrees every minute . " Because they have proportionally less surface area for cooling than smaller animals , thoroughbreds rely heavily on sweating - but blood flow to the skin drops sharply when galloping , prioritising the muscles instead . <p> This design helps a horse outrun predators , but creates a welfare problem in competition . " A horse can hit 43 ? C inside . It can tolerate that for a few minutes , but after 10-15 minutes , damage begins - to the brain , the gut , the muscles . " Heat stress can cause fatigue , collapse , abnormal heart rhythms , and , hours later , colic or laminitis @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ include long travel in warm weather , afternoon racing ( three times the risk compared to evenings ) , sudden temperature spikes without acclimatisation , longer or faster races , and soft going . " In Britain , we see around 36 recorded cases of heat-related issues in racing each year - but it 's often misdiagnosed , " he noted . " If a horse colics after a hot race , people do n't always connect the dots . " <p> His advice was unequivocal : cool first , ask questions later . " The single best thing you can do is get cold water on the horse , continuously , over the whole body . Forget scraping - you 're just wasting time . Do n't be afraid of overcooling . I 've yet to see a horse die from being cooled too much . " Walking the horse in wide circles keeps blood returning to the heart and aids recovery . <p> Marlin debunked common myths : cold water does not cause muscle damage , shock , laminitis , or heart attacks , and leaving a horse @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ , hoses , misting fans , ice - all have a role , but " nothing beats a steady flow of cold water " . <p> Prevention , he stressed , is equally important . " If you train early in the morning when it 's cool , then race in the heat , your horse is at a disadvantage and at greater risk . Two weeks of training in warmer parts of the day gives about 90% of heat acclimatisation . " <p> Other measures include rescheduling races to cooler times , avoiding dark artificial tracks on hot days , and monitoring environmental stress with a Wet Bulb Globe Temperature ( WBGT ) meter - " they cost a couple of hundred pounds and give you all the information you need " . <p> Beyond welfare , Marlin warned that visible heat distress damages public perception . " In the age of social media , the image of a wobbly horse standing in the sun while everyone else is celebrating is a gift to critics . Managing heat well is n't just about the horse - it 's about @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ closing message was clear and urgent : " Early recognition and decisive cooling save lives . There is no excuse not to get this right . " <p> AS the final session drew to a close , one thing was clear -- A Good Life - Well Lived Summit had delivered a wealth of practical , immediately applicable insights from some of the most knowledgeable voices in the field . <p> From nutritional science to welfare-led management strategies , the advice shared over the two days was rooted in real-world experience and backed by the latest research . Yet , while the content was exceptional , the attendance numbers from across the wider equine industry were not as high as they could -- and should -- have been . <p> If the sector is serious about advancing the welfare and performance of our horses , showing up to learn , engage , and apply this knowledge is not optional -- it 's a responsibility . Progress depends on open ears , open minds , and a shared willingness to put the horse first .
